I don’t know if I’d say I lose interest, exactly. Like, I still care about some things in theory, but when I’m actually doing them, my mind wanders off.

I’m trying to think of something specific, and honestly I don’t know if I have a clean example.

Like, I used to look forward to… I guess certain routines after work, but even saying that, I’m not sure if it was “enjoyment” exactly or just feeling like I was being productive. I’ve always been kind of bad at separating relaxing from, like, optimizing my time, which probably sounds ridiculous. Even when I’d watch something or meet someone, I’d be thinking about what I should be doing next.

So… I don’t know. I can’t really point to one hobby and say, “I used to love this and now I feel nothing.” It’s more vague than that. I notice I’m less absorbed, maybe, but I’m not sure whether that means I’m not enjoying it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I know you’re asking for something concrete, and I’m trying, but I still feel like I’m kind of reaching.

Maybe something small like watching a show after work, or going out to grab food with someone. I can do it, and sometimes it’s fine, but I don’t know if I’d call it pleasure exactly. It’s more like… I’m aware I’m supposed to be enjoying it, and part of me is checking whether I am.

And then I start thinking about other stuff, like whether I should be doing something more useful, or comparing where I am to where other people are. So it’s hard for me to tell if the activity itself isn’t enjoyable anymore or if I’m just not really present for it.

Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m bad at measuring it in neat blocks of time, like “this week was better” or whatever, because I’ll have days where work goes okay or someone is nice to me and I can sort of function normally.

More like two years and two months.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times during these two years when your mood has become significantly worse, with symptoms like losing interest in almost all activities, feeling hopeless, or having trouble sleeping or eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does get worse in this specific way where I just feel kind of hopeless. Like, I used to have these really detailed plans for getting an MBA and what steps I’d take at work, what roles I’d aim for next… I even had spreadsheets for it, which sounds very management analyst of me.\n\nBut lately I’ll look at the same plans and it’s like my brain just goes, “You’re not getting there.” Like no matter how much effort I put in, it won’t actually happen. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it has. Work is probably the clearest example, because it’s not just me being hard on myself anymore. My last performance review actually mentioned that I’m missing follow-through on things now, which was embarrassing because I used to be the person who kept track of all the little details.

One of my friends is already talking about grad school applications, and then I start comparing myself, and—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really sat down and thought, “I don’t enjoy anything anymore.” It’s more just… everything feels kind of muted, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: When your mood gets worse, do you find that you lose interest in activities you usually care about, even things like hobbies, spending time with friends, or work tasks that normally feel meaningful to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I lose interest, exactly. I’m not sure I can give you a clean example. Like, I know there probably are things, but when I try to compare “before” and “now,” it gets blurry.

I used to do a lot of normal stuff—watch shows, go out, play soccer sometimes—and I assume I enjoyed it more then, but I don’t know if I can point to one moment and say, yes, that was genuine enjoyment and now it’s gone.

Sometimes I wonder if I’m overthinking the question, because then I start thinking about whether anyone really enjoys things consistently, or if people just say they do. So… I guess I don’t have a specific clear example coming to mind right now.

You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… maybe? It’s hard for me to separate “lost interest” from just being tired of everything, if that makes sense.

Like, I’ll think about things I used to care about and I can still understand why they mattered. It’s not like I suddenly hate them. But the pull toward them isn’t really there in the same way. Or maybe I just don’t trust my own memory of how much I looked forward to things before.

I guess I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t have a specific thing where I can say, “yes, this mattered and now it doesn’t.” It’s more vague than that.

  {
    "role": "system",

Has it been at least two years, or more like a year or less?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been more than two years. I think about two years and a couple months now.\n\nIt kind of started after this dashboard project at work put me more in the spotlight, which sounds maybe not like a big deal, but… I don’t know, after that it just felt like this cloud settled in and never really left. So, yeah, not just a year or less. More like two years and two months.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times during these two years when your mood has become significantly worse, with symptoms like losing interest in almost all activities, feeling hopeless, or having trouble sleeping or eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does get worse in this specific way where I just feel kind of hopeless. Like, I used to have these really detailed plans for getting an MBA and what steps I’d take at work, what roles I’d aim for next… I even had spreadsheets for it, which sounds very management analyst of me.\n\nBut lately I’ll look at the same plans and it’s like my brain just goes, “You’re not getting there.” Like no matter how much effort I put in, it won’t actually happen. And then I get stuck thinking about that instead of doing anything useful.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest in almost all activities, even ones you used to enjoy, during these periods when your mood gets worse?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, “almost all activities” sounds… really broad. I don’t think I’ve noticed it in that clear-cut way.\n\nLike, I still do things sometimes, or I’ll start something because it’s part of my routine, but then I get caught up thinking about work stuff or whether I’m falling behind compared to people I know. One of my friends is already talking about grad school applications, and then I start comparing myself, and—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really sat down and thought, “I don’t enjoy anything anymore.” It’s more just… everything feels kind of muted, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: When your mood gets worse, do you find that you lose interest in activities you usually care about, even things like hobbies, spending time with friends, or work tasks that normally feel meaningful to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I lose interest, exactly. Like, I still care about some things in theory, but when I’m actually doing them, my mind wanders off.\n\nWith friends, for example, I’ll be there, but I’m not fully there, if that makes sense. And with work tasks, it’s weird because some of them used to feel like they mattered, like they were building toward something, but now I start thinking about whether I’m even on the right path, and then I’m not really focused on the task anymore.\n\nSorry, I feel like I’m not answering it cleanly. I guess I haven’t noticed a clear “I don’t care about anything” feeling. It’s more like I get distracted or kind of detached from things.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific activity or hobby that you used to enjoy before this feeling started—something you looked forward to doing—and whether you still feel any pleasure or satisfaction when you do it now?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something specific, and honestly I don’t know if I have a clean example.\n\nLike, I used to look forward to… I guess certain routines after work, but even saying that, I’m not sure if it was “enjoyment” exactly or just feeling like I was being productive. I’ve always been kind of bad at separating relaxing from, like, optimizing my time, which probably sounds ridiculous. Even when I’d watch something or meet someone, I’d be thinking about what I should be doing next.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really point to one hobby and say, “I used to love this and now I feel nothing.” It’s more vague than that. I notice I’m less absorbed, maybe, but I’m not sure whether that means I’m not enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a specific activity or hobby that you used to enjoy, even if it was small, and whether you still feel any sense of pleasure or satisfaction when you do it now?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking for something concrete, and I’m trying, but I still feel like I’m kind of reaching.\n\nMaybe something small like watching a show after work, or going out to grab food with someone. I can do it, and sometimes it’s fine, but I don’t know if I’d call it pleasure exactly. It’s more like… I’m aware I’m supposed to be enjoying it, and part of me is checking whether I am.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about other stuff, like whether I should be doing something more useful, or comparing where I am to where other people are. So it’s hard for me to tell if the activity itself isn’t enjoyable anymore or if I’m just not really present for it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your mood has been consistently low or empty most of the time over the past two years, without any significant periods where you felt clearly better for several weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m bad at measuring it in neat blocks of time, like “this week was better” or whatever, because I’ll have days where work goes okay or someone is nice to me and I can sort of function normally.\n\nBut underneath that, for a long time now, it’s like I’ve been walking around with this flat, heavy feeling. Kind of down, kind of empty most days. Even when nothing is specifically wrong, it’s still there. I don’t really remember a stretch where I felt clearly better for weeks, like genuinely lighter or back to myself.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your mood has made it hard for you to function at work or in your daily life, like trouble concentrating, making decisions, or keeping up with your usual responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. My last performance review actually mentioned that I’m missing follow-through on things now, which was embarrassing because I used to be the person who kept track of all the little details.\n\nAnd outside of work it’s kind of the same pattern. I’ve been dropping soccer commitments, and volunteering stuff I said I’d do, and even the MBA planning I used to be really on top of. I still think about all of it, but then I don’t move it forward, or I let something slip and then feel stupid about it.\n\nI don’t know, it’s like I can still show up enough that it doesn’t look dramatic from the outside, but I’m not keeping up the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the past when you genuinely enjoyed doing something, and now you don’t feel the same enjoyment when you do it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean example. Like, I know there probably are things, but when I try to compare “before” and “now,” it gets blurry.\n\nI used to do a lot of normal stuff—watch shows, go out, play soccer sometimes—and I assume I enjoyed it more then, but I don’t know if I can point to one moment and say, yes, that was genuine enjoyment and now it’s gone.\n\nSometimes I wonder if I’m overthinking the question, because then I start thinking about whether anyone really enjoys things consistently, or if people just say they do. It’s not like I suddenly hate them. But the pull toward them isn’t really there in the same way. Or maybe I just don’t trust my own memory of how much I looked forward to things before.\n\nI guess I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t have a specific thing where I can say, “yes, this mattered and now it doesn’t.” It’s more vague than that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like there are things you used to care about or look forward to in the past that just don’t seem to matter as much to you now, even if you can’t point to one specific thing?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 22 / management_analyst / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… maybe, but I don’t know how to say yes without feeling like I’m making it sound more definite than it is.

Like, there are things I used to think about more, or plan around more, and now they kind of sit in the background. But then I start wondering if that’s just normal, you know? Like when you get busy, or you’re not in college the same way anymore, or people’s priorities shift. I had a friend who got really into running for a while and then just stopped, and nobody treated that like some big meaningful thing.

So with me, I don’t know. I can tell there’s less… energy behind some things, maybe. But whether they “don’t matter” now, I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF34.1, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Name]. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I came in because I’ve just had this feeling for a long time, like I’m walking around with this heavy, flat kind of mood. Even when work is going fine—I'm a management analyst, so there are days where things technically go okay—or people are being nice, I still feel down. Kind of empty most days.\n\nI kept thinking maybe it was just stress or, I don’t know, needing a weekend, but it doesn’t really lift the way I expect it to.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way most days? Has it been at least two years, or more like a year or less?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been more than two years. I think about two years and a couple months now.\n\nIt kind of started after this dashboard project at work put me more in the spotlight, which sounds maybe not like a big deal, but… I don’t know, after that it just felt like this cloud settled in and never really left. So, yeah, not just a year or less. More like two years and two months.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times during these two years when your mood has become significantly worse, with symptoms like losing interest in almost all activities, feeling hopeless, or having trouble sleeping or eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does get worse in this specific way where I just feel kind of hopeless. Like, I used to have these really detailed plans for getting an MBA and what steps I’d take at work, what roles I’d aim for next… I even had spreadsheets for it, which sounds very management analyst of me.\n\nBut lately I’ll look at the same plans and it’s like my brain just goes, “You’re not getting there.” Like no matter how much effort I put in, it won’t actually happen. And then I get stuck thinking about that instead of doing anything useful.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest in almost all activities, even ones you used to enjoy, during these periods when your mood gets worse?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, “almost all activities” sounds… really broad. I don’t think I’ve noticed it in that clear-cut way.\n\nLike, I still do things sometimes, or I’ll start something because it’s part of my routine, but then I get caught up thinking about work stuff or whether I’m falling behind compared to people I know. One of my friends is already talking about grad school applications, and then I start comparing myself, and—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really sat down and thought, “I don’t enjoy anything anymore.” It’s more just… everything feels kind of muted, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: When your mood gets worse, do you find that you lose interest in activities you usually care about, even things like hobbies, spending time with friends, or work tasks that normally feel meaningful to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I lose interest, exactly. Like, I still care about some things in theory, but when I’m actually doing them, my mind wanders off.\n\nWith friends, for example, I’ll be there, but I’m not fully there, if that makes sense. And with work tasks, it’s weird because some of them used to feel like they mattered, like they were building toward something, but now I start thinking about whether I’m even on the right path, and then I’m not really focused on the task anymore.\n\nSorry, I feel like I’m not answering it cleanly. I guess I haven’t noticed a clear “I don’t care about anything” feeling. It’s more like I get distracted or kind of detached from things.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific activity or hobby that you used to enjoy before this feeling started—something you looked forward to doing—and whether you still feel any pleasure or satisfaction when you do it now?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something specific, and honestly I don’t know if I have a clean example.\n\nLike, I used to look forward to… I guess certain routines after work, but even saying that, I’m not sure if it was “enjoyment” exactly or just feeling like I was being productive. I’ve always been kind of bad at separating relaxing from, like, optimizing my time, which probably sounds ridiculous. Even when I’d watch something or meet someone, I’d be thinking about what I should be doing next.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really point to one hobby and say, “I used to love this and now I feel nothing.” It’s more vague than that. I notice I’m less absorbed, maybe, but I’m not sure whether that means I’m not enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a specific activity or hobby that you used to enjoy, even if it was small, and whether you still feel any sense of pleasure or satisfaction when you do it now?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking for something concrete, and I’m trying, but I still feel like I’m kind of reaching.\n\nMaybe something small like watching a show after work, or going out to grab food with someone. I can do it, and sometimes it’s fine, but I don’t know if I’d call it pleasure exactly. It’s more like… I’m aware I’m supposed to be enjoying it, and part of me is checking whether I am.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about other stuff, like whether I should be doing something more useful, or comparing where I am to where other people are. So it’s hard for me to tell if the activity itself isn’t enjoyable anymore or if I’m just not really present for it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your mood has been consistently low or empty most of the time over the past two years, without any significant periods where you felt clearly better for several weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m bad at measuring it in neat blocks of time, like “this week was better” or whatever, because I’ll have days where work goes okay or someone is nice to me and I can sort of function normally.\n\nBut underneath that, for a long time now, it’s like I’ve been walking around with this flat, heavy feeling. Kind of down, kind of empty most days. Even when nothing is specifically wrong, it’s still there. I don’t really remember a stretch where I felt clearly better for weeks, like genuinely lighter or back to myself.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your mood has made it hard for you to function at work or in your daily life, like trouble concentrating, making decisions, or keeping up with your usual responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. Work is probably the clearest example, because it’s not just me being hard on myself anymore. My last performance review actually mentioned that I’m missing follow-through on things now, which was embarrassing because I used to be the person who kept track of all the little details.\n\nAnd outside of work it’s kind of the same pattern. I’ve been dropping soccer commitments, and volunteering stuff I said I’d do, and even the MBA planning I used to be really on top of. I still think about all of it, but then I don’t move it forward, or I let something slip and then feel stupid about it.\n\nI don’t know, it’s like I can still show up enough that it doesn’t look dramatic from the outside, but I’m not keeping up the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the past when you genuinely enjoyed doing something, and now you don’t feel the same enjoyment when you do it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean example. Like, I know there probably are things, but when I try to compare “before” and “now,” it gets blurry.\n\nI used to do a lot of normal stuff—watch shows, go out, play soccer sometimes—and I assume I enjoyed it more then, but I don’t know if I can point to one moment and say, yes, that was genuine enjoyment and now it’s gone.\n\nSometimes I wonder if I’m overthinking the question, because then I start thinking about whether anyone really enjoys things consistently, or if people just say they do. So… I guess I don’t have a specific clear example coming to mind right now.\n\nDoctor: Even if it's not a specific hobby or activity you can name, do you feel like you've lost interest in things that used to matter to you, or that you looked forward to in the past?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? It’s hard for me to separate “lost interest” from just being tired of everything, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll think about things I used to care about and I can still understand why they mattered. It’s not like I suddenly hate them. But the pull toward them isn’t really there in the same way. Or maybe I just don’t trust my own memory of how much I looked forward to things before.\n\nI guess I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t have a specific thing where I can say, “yes, this mattered and now it doesn’t.” It’s more vague than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there are things you used to care about or look forward to in the past that just don’t seem to matter as much to you now, even if you can’t point to one specific thing?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe, but I don’t know how to say yes without feeling like I’m making it sound more definite than it is.\n\nLike, there are things I used to think about more, or plan around more, and now they kind of sit in the background. But then I start wondering if that’s just normal, you know? Like when you get busy, or you’re not in college the same way anymore, or people’s priorities shift. I had a friend who got really into running for a while and then just stopped, and nobody treated that like some big meaningful thing.\n\nSo with me, I don’t know. I can tell there’s less… energy behind some things, maybe. But whether they “don’t matter” now, I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that you’ve stopped doing things that used to feel important or meaningful to you, even if they weren’t necessarily fun or pleasurable?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think that’s probably closer to it than “fun,” actually.

Like, work is the obvious one, I guess. My last performance review actually mentioned that I’m missing follow-through now, which was kind of embarrassing because I used to be the person who stayed on top of stuff. Not perfect, but reliable enough.

And outside of work, it’s not one dramatic thing, but I’ve been dropping soccer commitments, and I used to volunteer and keep track of MBA planning stuff—programs, deadlines, whatever—and now I just kind of let it slide. Which is weird because those things felt like they meant something before, not necessarily that they were fun every second.

I don’t know, I keep thinking maybe I’m just busy or maybe everyone falls off things after a while, but… yeah, I have stopped following through on things that used to feel important.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF34.1, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Name]. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I came in because I’ve just had this feeling for a long time, like I’m walking around with this heavy, flat kind of mood. Even when work is going fine—I'm a management analyst, so there are days where things technically go okay—or people are being nice, I still feel down. Kind of empty most days.\n\nI kept thinking maybe it was just stress or, I don’t know, needing a weekend, but it doesn’t really lift the way I expect it to.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way most days? Has it been at least two years, or more like a year or less?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been more than two years. I think about two years and a couple months now.\n\nIt kind of started after this dashboard project at work put me more in the spotlight, which sounds maybe not like a big deal, but… I don’t know, after that it just felt like this cloud settled in and never really left. So, yeah, not just a year or less. More like two years and two months.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times during these two years when your mood has become significantly worse, with symptoms like losing interest in almost all activities, feeling hopeless, or having trouble sleeping or eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does get worse in this specific way where I just feel kind of hopeless. Like, I used to have these really detailed plans for getting an MBA and what steps I’d take at work, what roles I’d aim for next… I even had spreadsheets for it, which sounds very management analyst of me.\n\nBut lately I’ll look at the same plans and it’s like my brain just goes, “You’re not getting there.” Like no matter how much effort I put in, it won’t actually happen. And then I get stuck thinking about that instead of doing anything useful.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest in almost all activities, even ones you used to enjoy, during these periods when your mood gets worse?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, “almost all activities” sounds… really broad. I don’t think I’ve noticed it in that clear-cut way.\n\nLike, I still do things sometimes, or I’ll start something because it’s part of my routine, but then I get caught up thinking about work stuff or whether I’m falling behind compared to people I know. One of my friends is already talking about grad school applications, and then I start comparing myself, and—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really sat down and thought, “I don’t enjoy anything anymore.” It’s more just… everything feels kind of muted, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: When your mood gets worse, do you find that you lose interest in activities you usually care about, even things like hobbies, spending time with friends, or work tasks that normally feel meaningful to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I lose interest, exactly. Like, I still care about some things in theory, but when I’m actually doing them, my mind wanders off.\n\nWith friends, for example, I’ll be there, but I’m not fully there, if that makes sense. And with work tasks, it’s weird because some of them used to feel like they mattered, like they were building toward something, but now I start thinking about whether I’m even on the right path, and then I’m not really focused on the task anymore.\n\nSorry, I feel like I’m not answering it cleanly. I guess I haven’t noticed a clear “I don’t care about anything” feeling. It’s more like I get distracted or kind of detached from things.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific activity or hobby that you used to enjoy before this feeling started—something you looked forward to doing—and whether you still feel any pleasure or satisfaction when you do it now?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something specific, and honestly I don’t know if I have a clean example.\n\nLike, I used to look forward to… I guess certain routines after work, but even saying that, I’m not sure if it was “enjoyment” exactly or just feeling like I was being productive. I’ve always been kind of bad at separating relaxing from, like, optimizing my time, which probably sounds ridiculous. Even when I’d watch something or meet someone, I’d be thinking about what I should be doing next.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really point to one hobby and say, “I used to love this and now I feel nothing.” It’s more vague than that. I notice I’m less absorbed, maybe, but I’m not sure whether that means I’m not enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a specific activity or hobby that you used to enjoy, even if it was small, and whether you still feel any sense of pleasure or satisfaction when you do it now?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking for something concrete, and I’m trying, but I still feel like I’m kind of reaching.\n\nMaybe something small like watching a show after work, or going out to grab food with someone. I can do it, and sometimes it’s fine, but I don’t know if I’d call it pleasure exactly. It’s more like… I’m aware I’m supposed to be enjoying it, and part of me is checking whether I am.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about other stuff, like whether I should be doing something more useful, or comparing where I am to where other people are. So it’s hard for me to tell if the activity itself isn’t enjoyable anymore or if I’m just not really present for it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your mood has been consistently low or empty most of the time over the past two years, without any significant periods where you felt clearly better for several weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m bad at measuring it in neat blocks of time, like “this week was better” or whatever, because I’ll have days where work goes okay or someone is nice to me and I can sort of function normally.\n\nBut underneath that, for a long time now, it’s like I’ve been walking around with this flat, heavy feeling. Kind of down, kind of empty most days. Even when nothing is specifically wrong, it’s still there. I don’t really remember a stretch where I felt clearly better for weeks, like genuinely lighter or back to myself.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your mood has made it hard for you to function at work or in your daily life, like trouble concentrating, making decisions, or keeping up with your usual responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean… the MBA thing is probably the clearest example. I used to actually sit down and map it out—like which programs, what steps I’d need, what kind of career move would make sense before applying. I had spreadsheets and notes and all that.

Which is funny because I was never someone who loved doing applications or planning, but it felt like it was going somewhere. Like there was a path, even if it was annoying.

Now when I look at the same stuff, it just feels… kind of pointless? Not because I don’t technically still want it, but because I kind of assume I’m not going to get there anyway. Like no matter how much effort I put in, I’ll still fall short somehow. So the motivation just drains out before I even start.

I think about two years and a couple months now.\n\nIt kind of started after this dashboard project at work put me more in the spotlight, which sounds maybe not like a big deal, but… I don’t know, after that it just felt like this cloud settled in and never really left. So, yeah, not just a year or less. More like two years and two months.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times during these two years when your mood has become significantly worse, with symptoms like losing interest in almost all activities, feeling hopeless, or having trouble sleeping or eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does get worse in this specific way where I just feel kind of hopeless. Like, I used to have these really detailed plans for getting an MBA and what steps I’d take at work, what roles I’d aim for next… I even had spreadsheets for it, which sounds very management analyst of me.\n\nBut lately I’ll look at the same plans and it’s like my brain just goes, “You’re not getting there.” Like no matter how much effort I put in, it won’t actually happen. And then I get stuck thinking about that instead of doing anything useful.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest in almost all activities, even ones you used to enjoy, during these periods when your mood gets worse?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, “almost all activities” sounds… really broad. I don’t think I’ve noticed it in that clear-cut way.\n\nLike, I still do things sometimes, or I’ll start something because it’s part of my routine, but then I get caught up thinking about work stuff or whether I’m falling behind compared to people I know. One of my friends is already talking about grad school applications, and then I start comparing myself, and—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really sat down and thought, “I don’t enjoy anything anymore.” It’s more just… everything feels kind of muted, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: When your mood gets worse, do you find that you lose interest in activities you usually care about, even things like hobbies, spending time with friends, or work tasks that normally feel meaningful to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I lose interest, exactly. Like, I still care about some things in theory, but when I’m actually doing them, my mind wanders off.\n\nWith friends, for example, I’ll be there, but I’m not fully there, if that makes sense. And with work tasks, it’s weird because some of them used to feel like they mattered, like they were building toward something, but now I start thinking about whether I’m even on the right path, and then I’m not really focused on the task anymore.\n\nSorry, I feel like I’m not answering it cleanly. I guess I haven’t noticed a clear “I don’t care about anything” feeling. It’s more like I get distracted or kind of detached from things.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific activity or hobby that you used to enjoy before this feeling started—something you looked forward to doing—and whether you still feel any pleasure or satisfaction when you do it now?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something specific, and honestly I don’t know if I have a clean example.\n\nLike, I used to look forward to… I guess certain routines after work, but even saying that, I’m not sure if it was “enjoyment” exactly or just feeling like I was being productive. I’ve always been kind of bad at separating relaxing from, like, optimizing my time, which probably sounds ridiculous. Even when I’d watch something or meet someone, I’d be thinking about what I should be doing next.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really point to one hobby and say, “I used to love this and now I feel nothing.” It’s more vague than that. I notice I’m less absorbed, maybe, but I’m not sure whether that means I’m not enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a specific activity or hobby that you used to enjoy, even if it was small, and whether you still feel any sense of pleasure or satisfaction when you do it now?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking for something concrete, and I’m trying, but I still feel like I’m kind of reaching.\n\nMaybe something small like watching a show after work, or going out to grab food with someone. I can do it, and sometimes it’s fine, but I don’t know if I’d call it pleasure exactly. It’s more like… I’m aware I’m supposed to be enjoying it, and part of me is checking whether I am.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about other stuff, like whether I should be doing something more useful, or comparing where I am to where other people are. So it’s hard for me to tell if the activity itself isn’t enjoyable anymore or if I’m just not really present for it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your mood has been consistently low or empty most of the time over the past two years, without any significant periods where you felt clearly better for several weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m bad at measuring it in neat blocks of time, like “this week was better” or whatever, because I’ll have days where work goes okay or someone is nice to me and I can sort of function normally.\n\nBut underneath that, for a long time now, it’s like I’ve been walking around with this flat, heavy feeling. Kind of down, kind of empty most days. Even when nothing is specifically wrong, it’s still there. I don’t really remember a stretch where I felt clearly better for weeks, like genuinely lighter or back to myself.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your mood has made it hard for you to function at work or in your daily life, like trouble concentrating, making decisions, or keeping up with your usual responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. Work is probably the clearest example, because it’s not just me being hard on myself anymore. My last performance review actually mentioned that I’m missing follow-through on things now, which was embarrassing because I used to be the person who kept track of all the little details.\n\nAnd outside of work it’s kind of the same pattern. I’ve been dropping soccer commitments, and volunteering stuff I said I’d do, and even the MBA planning I used to be really on top of. I still think about all of it, but then I don’t move it forward, or I let something slip and then feel stupid about it.\n\nI don’t know, it’s like I can still show up enough that it doesn’t look dramatic from the outside, but I’m not keeping up the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the past when you genuinely enjoyed doing something, and now you don’t feel the same enjoyment when you do it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean example. Like, I know there probably are things, but when I try to compare “before” and “now,” it gets blurry.\n\nI used to do a lot of normal stuff—watch shows, go out, play soccer sometimes—and I assume I enjoyed it more then, but I don’t know if I can point to one moment and say, yes, that was genuine enjoyment and now it’s gone.\n\nSometimes I wonder if I’m overthinking the question, because then I start thinking about whether anyone really enjoys things consistently, or if people just say they do. So… I guess I don’t have a specific clear example coming to mind right now.\n\nDoctor: Even if it's not a specific hobby or activity you can name, do you feel like you've lost interest in things that used to matter to you, or that you looked forward to in the past?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? It’s hard for me to separate “lost interest” from just being tired of everything, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll think about things I used to care about and I can still understand why they mattered. It’s not like I suddenly hate them. But the pull toward them isn’t really there in the same way. Or maybe I just don’t trust my own memory of how much I looked forward to things before.\n\nI guess I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t have a specific thing where I can say, “yes, this mattered and now it doesn’t.” It’s more vague than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there are things you used to care about or look forward to in the past that just don’t seem to matter as much to you now, even if you can’t point to one specific thing?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe, but I don’t know how to say yes without feeling like I’m making it sound more definite than it is.\n\nLike, there are things I used to think about more, or plan around more, and now they kind of sit in the background. I had a friend who got really into running for a while and then just stopped, and nobody treated that like some big meaningful thing.\n\nSo with me, I don’t know. But whether they “don’t matter” now, I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve stopped doing things that used to feel important or meaningful to you, even if they weren’t necessarily fun or pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably closer to it than “fun,” actually.\n\nLike, work is the obvious one, I guess. Yeah… I think the MBA planning had that feeling for me. Not fun, exactly — honestly a lot of it was just looking up programs and requirements and trying to figure out career steps, which is kind of tedious — but it felt like it meant something. Like I was building toward a version of my life that made sense.

I used to get pretty detailed with it, too. I’d map things out, compare programs, think through what jobs would help me get there. I had this whole thing where I’d make notes and then reorganize the notes, which probably wasn’t even necessary. I do that with stuff sometimes, like I’ll spend more time making the system than using it.

But now when I look at the same plans, it doesn’t really feel purposeful in the same way. It’s more like… I already assume I’m not going to get there, no matter how much I try. So even if the goal still sounds like something I should want, it feels kind of hollow to work on it. Like I’m just pretending there’s a path when I don’t actually believe I’ll make it to the end.

Even when I’d watch something or meet someone, I’d be thinking about what I should be doing next.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I notice I’m less absorbed, maybe, but I’m not sure whether that means I’m not enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a specific activity or hobby that you used to enjoy, even if it was small, and whether you still feel any sense of pleasure or satisfaction when you do it now?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking for something concrete, and I’m trying, but I still feel like I’m kind of reaching.\n\nMaybe something small like watching a show after work, or going out to grab food with someone. I can do it, and sometimes it’s fine, but I don’t know if I’d call it pleasure exactly. It’s more like… I’m aware I’m supposed to be enjoying it, and part of me is checking whether I am.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about other stuff, like whether I should be doing something more useful, or comparing where I am to where other people are. So it’s hard for me to tell if the activity itself isn’t enjoyable anymore or if I’m just not really present for it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your mood has been consistently low or empty most of the time over the past two years, without any significant periods where you felt clearly better for several weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m bad at measuring it in neat blocks of time, like “this week was better” or whatever, because I’ll have days where work goes okay or someone is nice to me and I can sort of function normally.\n\nBut underneath that, for a long time now, it’s like I’ve been walking around with this flat, heavy feeling. Kind of down, kind of empty most days. Even when nothing is specifically wrong, it’s still there. I don’t really remember a stretch where I felt clearly better for weeks, like genuinely lighter or back to myself.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your mood has made it hard for you to function at work or in your daily life, like trouble concentrating, making decisions, or keeping up with your usual responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. Work is probably the clearest example, because it’s not just me being hard on myself anymore. My last performance review actually mentioned that I’m missing follow-through on things now, which was embarrassing because I used to be the person who kept track of all the little details.\n\nAnd outside of work it’s kind of the same pattern. I’ve been dropping soccer commitments, and volunteering stuff I said I’d do, and even the MBA planning I used to be really on top of. I still think about all of it, but then I don’t move it forward, or I let something slip and then feel stupid about it.\n\nI don’t know, it’s like I can still show up enough that it doesn’t look dramatic from the outside, but I’m not keeping up the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the past when you genuinely enjoyed doing something, and now you don’t feel the same enjoyment when you do it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean example. Like, I know there probably are things, but when I try to compare “before” and “now,” it gets blurry.\n\nI used to do a lot of normal stuff—watch shows, go out, play soccer sometimes—and I assume I enjoyed it more then, but I don’t know if I can point to one moment and say, yes, that was genuine enjoyment and now it’s gone.\n\nSometimes I wonder if I’m overthinking the question, because then I start thinking about whether anyone really enjoys things consistently, or if people just say they do. So… I guess I don’t have a specific clear example coming to mind right now.\n\nDoctor: Even if it's not a specific hobby or activity you can name, do you feel like you've lost interest in things that used to matter to you, or that you looked forward to in the past?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? It’s hard for me to separate “lost interest” from just being tired of everything, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll think about things I used to care about and I can still understand why they mattered. It’s not like I suddenly hate them. But the pull toward them isn’t really there in the same way. Or maybe I just don’t trust my own memory of how much I looked forward to things before.\n\nI guess I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t have a specific thing where I can say, “yes, this mattered and now it doesn’t.” It’s more vague than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there are things you used to care about or look forward to in the past that just don’t seem to matter as much to you now, even if you can’t point to one specific thing?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe, but I don’t know how to say yes without feeling like I’m making it sound more definite than it is.\n\nLike, there are things I used to think about more, or plan around more, and now they kind of sit in the background. But then I start wondering if that’s just normal, you know? Like when you get busy, or you’re not in college the same way anymore, or people’s priorities shift. I had a friend who got really into running for a while and then just stopped, and nobody treated that like some big meaningful thing.\n\nSo with me, I don’t know. I can tell there’s less… energy behind some things, maybe. But whether they “don’t matter” now, I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve stopped doing things that used to feel important or meaningful to you, even if they weren’t necessarily fun or pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably closer to it than “fun,” actually.\n\nLike, work is the obvious one, I guess. My last performance review actually mentioned that I’m missing follow-through now, which was kind of embarrassing because I used to be the person who stayed on top of stuff. Not perfect, but reliable enough.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s not one dramatic thing, but I’ve been dropping soccer commitments, and I used to volunteer and keep track of MBA planning stuff—programs, deadlines, whatever—and now I just kind of let it slide. Which is weird because those things felt like they meant something before, not necessarily that they were fun every second.\n\nI don’t know, I keep thinking maybe I’m just busy or maybe everyone falls off things after a while, but… yeah, I have stopped following through on things that used to feel important.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you felt motivated to work toward a goal that mattered to you, even if it wasn’t fun, and whether you still feel that same kind of motivation now?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the MBA thing is probably the clearest example. I used to actually sit down and map it out—like which programs, what steps I’d need, what kind of career move would make sense before applying. I had spreadsheets and notes and all that.\n\nWhich is funny because I was never someone who loved doing applications or planning, but it felt like it was going somewhere. Like there was a path, even if it was annoying.\n\nNow when I look at the same stuff, it just feels… kind of pointless? Not because I don’t technically still want it, but because I kind of assume I’m not going to get there anyway. Like no matter how much effort I put in, I’ll still fall short somehow. So the motivation just drains out before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt a sense of purpose or meaning in doing something, even if it wasn’t necessarily fun, and whether you still feel that same sense of purpose or meaning when doing it now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the MBA planning had that feeling for me. Not fun, exactly — honestly a lot of it was just looking up programs and requirements and trying to figure out career steps, which is kind of tedious — but it felt like it meant something. Like I was building toward a version of my life that made sense.\n\nI used to get pretty detailed with it, too. I’d map things out, compare programs, think through what jobs would help me get there. I had this whole thing where I’d make notes and then reorganize the notes, which probably wasn’t even necessary. I do that with stuff sometimes, like I’ll spend more time making the system than using it.\n\nBut now when I look at the same plans, it doesn’t really feel purposeful in the same way. It’s more like… I already assume I’m not going to get there, no matter how much I try. So even if the goal still sounds like something I should want, it feels kind of hollow to work on it. Like I’m just pretending there’s a path when I don’t actually believe I’ll make it to the end.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve stopped doing things that used to feel important or meaningful to you, even if they weren’t necessarily fun or pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably closer to it than “fun,” actually.\n\nLike, work is the obvious one, I guess. My last performance review actually mentioned that I’m missing follow-through now, which was kind of embarrassing because I used to be the person who stayed on top of stuff. Not perfect, but reliable enough.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s not one dramatic thing, but I’ve been dropping soccer commitments, and I used to volunteer and keep track of MBA planning stuff—programs, deadlines, whatever—and now I just kind of let it slide. Which is weird because those things felt like they meant something before, not necessarily that they were fun every second.\n\nI don’t know, I keep thinking maybe I’m just busy or maybe everyone falls off things after a while, but… yeah, I have stopped following through on things that used to feel important.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you felt motivated to work toward a goal that mattered to you, even if it wasn’t fun, and whether you still feel that same kind of motivation now?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the MBA thing is probably the clearest example. I used to actually sit down and map it out—like which programs, what steps I’d need, what kind of career move would make sense before applying. I had spreadsheets and notes and all that.\n\nWhich is funny because I was never someone who loved doing applications or planning, but it felt like it was going somewhere. Like there was a path, even if it was annoying.\n\nNow when I look at the same stuff, it just feels… kind of pointless? People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not completely, I guess… I mean, sometimes I can still sort of remember why it mattered. Like if I’m looking at a school website or reading about a career path, there’s maybe a second where I’m like, “Yeah, that was the plan.”

But then pretty quickly I just… lose it. I’ll look at the same MBA notes or career steps I used to map out in detail, and instead of feeling motivated, I just assume I’m not actually going to get there no matter how much I try.

It’s kind of like when you open a bunch of tabs intending to do something productive, and then suddenly there are twenty tabs and none of them make sense anymore. That’s probably a weird comparison, but yeah — the purpose comes up briefly, maybe, but it doesn’t really stick.

More like two years and two months.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times during these two years when your mood has become significantly worse, with symptoms like losing interest in almost all activities, feeling hopeless, or having trouble sleeping or eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does get worse in this specific way where I just feel kind of hopeless. Like, I used to have these really detailed plans for getting an MBA and what steps I’d take at work, what roles I’d aim for next… I even had spreadsheets for it, which sounds very management analyst of me.\n\nBut lately I’ll look at the same plans and it’s like my brain just goes, “You’re not getting there.” Like no matter how much effort I put in, it won’t actually happen. And then I get stuck thinking about that instead of doing anything useful.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest in almost all activities, even ones you used to enjoy, during these periods when your mood gets worse?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. One of my friends is already talking about grad school applications, and then I start comparing myself, and—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really sat down and thought, “I don’t enjoy anything anymore.” It’s more just… everything feels kind of muted, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: When your mood gets worse, do you find that you lose interest in activities you usually care about, even things like hobbies, spending time with friends, or work tasks that normally feel meaningful to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I lose interest, exactly. I guess I haven’t noticed a clear “I don’t care about anything” feeling. It’s more like I get distracted or kind of detached from things.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific activity or hobby that you used to enjoy before this feeling started—something you looked forward to doing—and whether you still feel any pleasure or satisfaction when you do it now?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something specific, and honestly I don’t know if I have a clean example.\n\nLike, I used to look forward to… I guess certain routines after work, but even saying that, I’m not sure if it was “enjoyment” exactly or just feeling like I was being productive. I’ve always been kind of bad at separating relaxing from, like, optimizing my time, which probably sounds ridiculous. So it’s hard for me to tell if the activity itself isn’t enjoyable anymore or if I’m just not really present for it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your mood has been consistently low or empty most of the time over the past two years, without any significant periods where you felt clearly better for several weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m bad at measuring it in neat blocks of time, like “this week was better” or whatever, because I’ll have days where work goes okay or someone is nice to me and I can sort of function normally.\n\nBut underneath that, for a long time now, it’s like I’ve been walking around with this flat, heavy feeling. So… I guess I don’t have a specific clear example coming to mind right now.\n\nDoctor: Even if it's not a specific hobby or activity you can name, do you feel like you've lost interest in things that used to matter to you, or that you looked forward to in the past?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I still think about all of it, but then I don’t move it forward, or I let something slip and then feel stupid about it.\n\nI don’t know, it’s like I can still show up enough that it doesn’t look dramatic from the outside, but I’m not keeping up the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the past when you genuinely enjoyed doing something, and now you don’t feel the same enjoyment when you do it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean example. Like, I know there probably are things, but when I try to compare “before” and “now,” it gets blurry.\n\nI used to do a lot of normal stuff—watch shows, go out, play soccer sometimes—and I assume I enjoyed it more then, but I don’t know if I can point to one moment and say, yes, that was genuine enjoyment and now it’s gone.\n\nSometimes I wonder if I’m overthinking the question, because then I start thinking about whether anyone really enjoys things consistently, or if people just say they do. So… I guess I don’t have a specific clear example coming to mind right now.\n\nDoctor: Even if it's not a specific hobby or activity you can name, do you feel like you've lost interest in things that used to matter to you, or that you looked forward to in the past?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? It’s hard for me to separate “lost interest” from just being tired of everything, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll think about things I used to care about and I can still understand why they mattered. It’s not like I suddenly hate them. But the pull toward them isn’t really there in the same way. Or maybe I just don’t trust my own memory of how much I looked forward to things before.\n\nI guess I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t have a specific thing where I can say, “yes, this mattered and now it doesn’t.” It’s more vague than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there are things you used to care about or look forward to in the past that just don’t seem to matter as much to you now, even if you can’t point to one specific thing?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe, but I don’t know how to say yes without feeling like I’m making it sound more definite than it is.\n\nLike, there are things I used to think about more, or plan around more, and now they kind of sit in the background. But then I start wondering if that’s just normal, you know? Like when you get busy, or you’re not in college the same way anymore, or people’s priorities shift. I had a friend who got really into running for a while and then just stopped, and nobody treated that like some big meaningful thing.\n\nSo with me, I don’t know. I can tell there’s less… energy behind some things, maybe. But whether they “don’t matter” now, I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve stopped doing things that used to feel important or meaningful to you, even if they weren’t necessarily fun or pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably closer to it than “fun,” actually.\n\nLike, work is the obvious one, I guess. My last performance review actually mentioned that I’m missing follow-through now, which was kind of embarrassing because I used to be the person who stayed on top of stuff. Not perfect, but reliable enough.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s not one dramatic thing, but I’ve been dropping soccer commitments, and I used to volunteer and keep track of MBA planning stuff—programs, deadlines, whatever—and now I just kind of let it slide. Which is weird because those things felt like they meant something before, not necessarily that they were fun every second.\n\nI don’t know, I keep thinking maybe I’m just busy or maybe everyone falls off things after a while, but… yeah, I have stopped following through on things that used to feel important.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you felt motivated to work toward a goal that mattered to you, even if it wasn’t fun, and whether you still feel that same kind of motivation now?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the MBA thing is probably the clearest example. I used to actually sit down and map it out—like which programs, what steps I’d need, what kind of career move would make sense before applying. I had spreadsheets and notes and all that.\n\nWhich is funny because I was never someone who loved doing applications or planning, but it felt like it was going somewhere. Like there was a path, even if it was annoying.\n\nNow when I look at the same stuff, it just feels… kind of pointless? Not because I don’t technically still want it, but because I kind of assume I’m not going to get there anyway. Like no matter how much effort I put in, I’ll still fall short somehow. So the motivation just drains out before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt a sense of purpose or meaning in doing something, even if it wasn’t necessarily fun, and whether you still feel that same sense of purpose or meaning when doing it now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the MBA planning had that feeling for me. Not fun, exactly — honestly a lot of it was just looking up programs and requirements and trying to figure out career steps, which is kind of tedious — but it felt like it meant something. Like I was building toward a version of my life that made sense.\n\nI used to get pretty detailed with it, too. I’d map things out, compare programs, think through what jobs would help me get there. I had this whole thing where I’d make notes and then reorganize the notes, which probably wasn’t even necessary. I do that with stuff sometimes, like I’ll spend more time making the system than using it.\n\nBut now when I look at the same plans, it doesn’t really feel purposeful in the same way. It’s more like… I already assume I’m not going to get there, no matter how much I try. So even if the goal still sounds like something I should want, it feels kind of hollow to work on it. Like I’m just pretending there’s a path when I don’t actually believe I’ll make it to the end.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely lost that sense of purpose or meaning in things you used to work toward, like the MBA planning, or does it still come up sometimes, even briefly?\n\nPatient: Not completely, I guess… I mean, sometimes I can still sort of remember why it mattered. Like if I’m looking at a school website or reading about a career path, there’s maybe a second where I’m like, “Yeah, that was the plan.”\n\nBut then pretty quickly I just… lose it. I’ll look at the same MBA notes or career steps I used to map out in detail, and instead of feeling motivated, I just assume I’m not actually going to get there no matter how much I try.\n\nIt’s kind of like when you open a bunch of tabs intending to do something productive, and then suddenly there are twenty tabs and none of them make sense anymore. That’s probably a weird comparison, but yeah — the purpose comes up briefly, maybe, but it doesn’t really stick."
